# Nayib Bukele

"Bukele" redirects here. For other people with this surname, see [Bukele (surname)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukele_\(surname\)).

In this name that follows [Hispanic American naming customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naming_customs_of_Hispanic_America), the first or paternal [surname](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surname) is&#x20;*&#x20;Bukele* and the second or maternal family name is&#x20;*&#x20;Ortez*.

**Nayib Armando Bukele Ortez**[^cite_note-3] (born 24 July 1981) is a Salvadoran politician who has served as the 43rd [president of El Salvador](466532.md) since 2019.

In 1999, Bukele established an advertising company and worked at another owned by his father, [Armando Bukele Kattán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armando_Bukele_Katt%C3%A1n). Both companies advertised election campaigns for the [Farabundo Martí National Liberation Front](327525.md) (FMLN) political party. Bukele entered politics in 2011. In 2012, he joined the FMLN and was elected mayor of [Nuevo Cuscatlán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuevo_Cuscatl%C3%A1n). Bukele served until his 2015 election as [Mayor of San Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_San_Salvador), where he served until 2018. In 2017, Bukele was ousted from the FMLN. He founded the [Nuevas Ideas](61703199.md) political party shortly afterward and pursued a presidential [campaign in 2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Salvadoran_presidential_election). After the [Supreme Electoral Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Electoral_Court_\(El_Salvador\)) (TSE) refused to register his party, Bukele ran for president with the [Grand Alliance for National Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Alliance_for_National_Unity) (GANA) and won with 53 percent of the vote.

In July 2019, Bukele implemented the [Territorial Control Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_Control_Plan) to reduce El Salvador's 2019 [homicide rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homicide_rate) of 38 per 100,000 people. Homicides fell by 50 percent during Bukele's first year in office. After 87 people were killed by gangs over one weekend in March 2022, Bukele initiated a [nationwide crackdown on gangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvadoran_gang_crackdown), resulting in the arrests of over 85,000 people with alleged gang affiliations by December 2024. El Salvador's homicide rate officially decreased to 1.9 homicides per 100,000 in 2024, one of the lowest in the [Americas](29833.md), although the factual accuracy has been contested.[^cite_note-4][^cite_note-5][^cite_note-6] Bukele passed a law in 2021 that made [bitcoin](28249265.md) [legal tender in El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitcoin_in_El_Salvador) and promoted plans to build [Bitcoin City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitcoin_City). In June 2023, the [Legislative Assembly](1608500.md) approved Bukele's proposals to reduce the number [of municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_El_Salvador) from 262 to 44 and the number of seats in the legislature from 84 to 60. He ran for re-election in the [2024 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Salvadoran_presidential_election) and won with 85 percent of the vote after the [Supreme Court of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Justice_of_El_Salvador) reinterpreted [the constitution](23491231.md)'s ban on consecutive re-election.

Bukele is highly popular in El Salvador and throughout [Latin America](18524.md). He holds an [average approval rating of 88.6%](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_on_the_Nayib_Bukele_presidency) during his entire presidency with approval ratings never having fallen below 75%. El Salvador has experienced [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding) under Bukele's leadership, falling 61 places in the [World Press Freedom Index](8264708.md) between 2019 and 2025 and 24 places in the [Economist Intelligence Unit's Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist_Democracy_Index), which now classifies El Salvador as a [hybrid regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_regime).[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-8] In February 2020, [Bukele ordered 40 soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Salvadoran_political_crisis) into the Legislative Assembly building to intimidate lawmakers into approving a [US\$](18717338.md)109 million loan for the Territorial Control Plan, an event that triggered a political crisis and was described by the opposition as a [self-coup](1221872.md). After [Nuevas Ideas](61703199.md) won a supermajority in the [2021 legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Salvadoran_legislative_election), Bukele's allies in the legislature voted to replace the [attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_of_El_Salvador) and all five justices of the Supreme Court of Justice's Constitutional Chamber. Bukele has criticized journalists and news outlets, and has furthered [press censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship). Following a controversial constitutional amendment on 31 July 2025, the Legislative Assembly enabled indefinite reelection, extended presidential terms from five to six years, and eliminated the [two-round system](25932.md).[^cite_note-9]

## Early life

Nayib Armando Bukele Ortez was born on 24 July 1981 in [San Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Salvador), El Salvador.[^cite_note-Revista_Elementos-10] His father was [Armando Bukele Kattán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armando_Bukele_Katt%C3%A1n), a businessman and industrial chemist,[^cite_note-TTOI-11][^cite_note-Oligarchs-12] and his mother is Olga Marina Ortez. Bukele's father died in 2015.[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] Bukele was the couple's first child. He has three younger brothers, [Karim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karim_Bukele), [Yusef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusef_Bukele), and [Ibrajim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrajim_Bukele), and has four paternal half-sisters and two paternal half-brothers.[^cite_note-Revista_Elementos-10][^cite_note-14] Bukele's father [converted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Islam) from [Christianity](5211.md) to [Islam](6037917.md) in the 1980s, became an [imam](15240.md), and founded four [mosques](19894.md) in El Salvador.[^cite_note-Oligarchs-12] Bukele's mother is [Catholic](606848.md).[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] Bukele's paternal grandparents were [Palestinian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christians) who emigrated to El Salvador from [Jerusalem](16043.md) and [Bethlehem](4312.md) in 1921. His maternal grandfather was [Greek Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church), and his maternal grandmother was Catholic.[^cite_note-TTOI-11]

Bukele completed his secondary education at the Escuela Panamericana in 1999 at age 18.[^cite_note-Oligarchs-12][^cite_note-CIDOB-13] Bukele enrolled at [Central American University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_American_University,_San_Salvador) in San Salvador to study legal sciences, aspiring to become a lawyer,[^cite_note-Oligarchs-12][^cite_note-CIDOB-13] but dropped out to work for the Nölck advertising agency, one of his father's businesses.[^cite_note-El_Espanol-15] Nölck campaigned for the [Farabundo Martí National Liberation Front](327525.md) (FMLN), a left-wing Salvadoran [political party](409225.md).[^cite_note-Bernal-16]

In 1999, Bukele founded the marketing company Obermet, also known as 4am Saatchi & Saatchi El Salvador, and was its president from 1999 to 2006 and from 2010 to 2012.[^cite_note-El_Espanol-15][^cite_note-17] The company ran political advertising for the FMLN presidential campaigns of [Schafik Hándal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schafik_H%C3%A1ndal) in [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Salvadoran_presidential_election) and [Mauricio Funes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauricio_Funes) in [2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Salvadoran_presidential_election).[^cite_note-CIDOB-13][^cite_note-Roque_Baldovinos-18]<sup>: 239 </sup> Bukele was president of Yamaha Motors El Salvador, a company that sells and distributes [Yamaha products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamaha_Motor_Company) in El Salvador, from 2009 to 2012.[^cite_note-EF1-19] During Bukele's business career, he called himself a "businessman with a great future" ("*empresario con gran futuro*").[^cite_note-Bernal-16]

## Early political career

### Mayor of Nuevo Cuscatlán

In 2011, Bukele announced that he would enter politics as a member of the FMLN[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] to break out of "his comfort zone" ("*su zona de confort*") as a businessman.[^cite_note-Bernal-16] Officially joining the party in 2012,[^cite_note-USDW-20] he campaigned for the mayoralty of [Nuevo Cuscatlán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuevo_Cuscatl%C3%A1n), a municipality in the department of [La Libertad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Libertad_Department_\(El_Salvador\)), part of the San Salvador metropolitan area. Bukele's campaign was supported by the [Democratic Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Change_\(El_Salvador\)) party.[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] He [was elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Salvadoran_legislative_and_local_elections) mayor of Nuevo Cuscatlán in March 2012 with 51.67 percent of the vote, defeating primary challenger Tomás Rodríguez of the [Nationalist Republican Alliance](542175.md) (ARENA).[^cite_note-TSE_2012-21] Bukele took office on 1 May 2012[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] as the country's youngest mayor.[^cite_note-Bernal-16]

Bukele created a scholarship program for youths in the municipality,[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] donating his \$2,000 salary to fund the program.[^cite_note-EF1-19] In August 2014, Bukele launched Sphere PM, a project that launched a [high-altitude balloon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-altitude_balloon) to an altitude of 100,000 feet (30,000 m) and took pictures of El Salvador.[^cite_note-22] He stated that Sphere PM's goal was to promote education in science and technology to dissuade the municipality's youth from crime.[^cite_note-23] Bukele spoke at the [United Nations headquarters](188955.md) about projects he had undertaken as mayor of Nuevo Cuscatlán as part of the November 2014 [World Cities Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Cities_Day).[^cite_note-24] In January 2015, he inaugurated a \$1.7 million boulevard connecting Nuevo Cuscatlán with [Huizúcar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huiz%C3%BAcar) and [Antiguo Cuscatlán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiguo_Cuscatl%C3%A1n).[^cite_note-25] Bukele did much of his mayoral work with funding from ALBA Petróleos, owned by the Venezuelan oil company [PDVSA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PDVSA).[^cite_note-Roque_Baldovinos-18]<sup>: 239 </sup>

### Mayor of San Salvador

In August 2014, Bukele announced that he would seek election as [mayor of San Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_San_Salvador) in the [2015 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Salvadoran_legislative_election).[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] His candidacy was confirmed by FMLN secretary-general [Medardo González](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medardo_Gonz%C3%A1lez) on 19 August 2014.[^cite_note-26] Bukele delegated administration of Nuevo Cuscatlán to council member [Michelle Sol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Sol) in February 2015 to focus on his campaign.[^cite_note-27] During his campaign, which was supported by the [Salvadoran Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvadoran_Progressive_Party?action=edit\&redlink=1), FMLN party leadership called Bukele the party's "crown jewel" ("*joya de la corona*"). Bukele's campaign used catchphrases such as "we have to change history" ("*tenemos que cambiar la historia*") and "together we will go forward" ("*juntos saldremos adelante*") to rally support from young voters.[^cite_note-Bernal-16]

His primary opponent was Edwin Zamora, a businessman and [Legislative Assembly](1608500.md) deputy from ARENA. Bukele led Zamora in opinion polls before the election.[^cite_note-Bernal-16] He defeated Zamora with 50.38 percent of the vote on 1 March 2015, and took office on 1 May.[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] Bukele appointed a cousin, Hassan, and his half-brother [Yamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamil_Bukele) to administrative positions on the San Salvador municipal council. The appointments were criticized by ARENA and FMLN politicians.[^cite_note-roads-28]

Nayib Bukele shaking hands with children dressed in bee costumes

Bukele at the opening of the La Colmenita children's theater, January 2016

A photograph of the mayors of several Ibero-American capital cities

At the 18th summit of the [Union of Ibero-American Capital Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Ibero-American_Capital_Cities), April 2018

As mayor, Bukele began a "reordering" ("*reordenamiento*") to revitalize the [city's historic downtown area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_Downtown_San_Salvador) and combat crime.[^cite_note-reordering-29] On the day Bukele took office, he reverted the names of two streets in San Salvador (Calle Mayor [Roberto D'Aubuisson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_D'Aubuisson) and Boulevard Coronel [José Arturo Castellanos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Arturo_Castellanos)) to Calle San Antonio Abad and Boulevard Venezuela, respectively. Both names had been changed by Bukele's predecessor, [Norman Quijano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Quijano). Zamora, who had become a member of San Salvador's municipal council, stated that the names were reverted due to flaws in the initial renaming process.[^cite_note-30] He added that another street would be named in honor of Castellanos, who provided fake Salvadoran passports to 40,000 Central European Jews to help them escape [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust).[^cite_note-roads-28] Bukele renamed 89 Avenida Norte in honor of Castellanos in June 2016.[^cite_note-31]

In December 2016, Bukele inaugurated the Cuscatlán Market to encourage [street vendors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawker_\(trade\)) to relocate their businesses.[^cite_note-32] Many vendors refused to move, despite the market.[^cite_note-reordering-29] Political opponents and investigative journalists accused him of negotiating with gangs to organize its construction, since it was located in gang-controlled territory.[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] In January 2016, Bukele began a "San Salvador 100% Illuminated" campaign to "have a light on every corner of San Salvador" to combat crime in the city. The campaign was completed by May 2016.[^cite_note-reordering-29] He also installed video-surveillance cameras in parts of San Salvador that were severely affected by crime.[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] Bukele inaugurated the renovated downtown [Gerardo Barrios Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaza_Gerardo_Barrios) in October 2017,[^cite_note-33] and the new downtown Lineal Plaza in April 2018.[^cite_note-34]

Bukele created a scholarship program, known as the Dalton Project and funded by his salary,[^cite_note-El_Espanol-15] for youth in San Salvador to prevent them from joining gangs.[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] Bukele also created the My New School project to modernize San Salvador's primary schools.[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] In May 2015, he signed an agreement with [Panama City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_City) mayor [José Blandón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Isabel_Bland%C3%B3n_Figueroa) to establish a [sister city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_city) relationship between San Salvador and Panama City.[^cite_note-35] In November 2015, Bukele signed an agreement with the Spanish [National League of Professional Football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liga_Nacional_de_F%C3%BAtbol_Profesional) to promote sports for San Salvador's youth.[^cite_note-36]

In September 2016, Bukele visited [Washington, D.C.](108956.md) and met with Mayor [Muriel Bowser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muriel_Bowser) to discuss the implementation of urban-development projects. Bukele received the [keys to the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keys_to_the_city) of [Gaithersburg, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaithersburg,_Maryland), and 11 September was designated the "Day of Mayor Nayib Bukele" ("*Día del alcalde Nayib Bukele*").[^cite_note-37] He visited [Taipei](57648.md) in February 2017 and met with Taiwanese president [Tsai Ing-wen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsai_Ing-wen) to enhance the sister-city relationship between San Salvador and Taipei.[^cite_note-38] In February 2018, Bukele attended the 32nd International Mayors Conference in Jerusalem[^cite_note-TTOI-11] and prayed at the [Western Wall](75225.md).[^cite_note-39]

### Troll Center case

In January 2016, the *[El Diario de Hoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Diario_de_Hoy)* and *[La Prensa Gráfica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Prensa_Gr%C3%A1fica)* newspapers reported that the Búnker digital-programming company had created [mirror sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirror_site) of the newspapers in June 2015 and posted [false information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news) in an attempt to damage their reputations; the newspapers described the incident as a [cyberattack](37925700.md). In a subsequent investigation by the office of the attorney general (FGR), Bukele allegedly instructed a [Twitter](9988187.md) user to create the mirror sites. Bukele denied involvement in the creation of the mirror sites.[^cite_note-TCC-40][^cite_note-41] The incident became known as the "Troll Center" case.[^cite_note-TCC-40] Five people were charged in relation to the case, but the charges were dropped in December 2017.[^cite_note-42]

On 4 July 2017, Bukele sued *La Prensa Gráfica* for \$6 million, alleging that the newspaper had defamed and slandered him in its reporting of the cyberattacks by "falsely" ("*falsamente*") connecting him to the Troll Center case and "damag\[ing] \[Bukele's] image" ("*dañó la imagen del señor alcalde*"). Later that month, a court dismissed Bukele's lawsuit and three other courts rejected his appeals.[^cite_note-43] In December 2018, the FGR stated that it had reviewed information supposedly linking Bukele's cell phone to the cyberattacks.[^cite_note-44]

### Expulsion from the FMLN

A color photograph of Salvador Sánchez Cerén and Nayib Bukele shaking hands

Then-mayor Bukele with President [Salvador Sánchez Cerén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_S%C3%A1nchez_Cer%C3%A9n) of the FMLN, May 2015

Bukele's relationship with the FMLN began to deteriorate after he became mayor of San Salvador.[^cite_note-CNN_FMLN-45] He clashed with other party members on Twitter,[^cite_note-CIDOB-13] and frequently resisted FMLN party leadership.[^cite_note-Roque_Baldovinos-18]<sup>: 239 </sup> Bukele became a strong critic of [Salvador Sánchez Cerén](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_S%C3%A1nchez_Cer%C3%A9n), the FMLN [president of El Salvador](466532.md) who was elected in 2014.[^cite_note-CNN_FMLN-45] He threatened to leave the party in 2015 if the FMLN-led government reappointed Luis Martínez as the country's [attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_of_El_Salvador), describing Martínez as "a gangster, very corrupt, \[and] the worst of the worst". The FMLN relented and replaced Martínez, and Bukele later admitted that his threat to leave the party "was a bluff".[^cite_note-reordering-29]

In September 2017, San Salvador FMLN member Xóchitl Marchelli alleged that Bukele had thrown an apple at her, calling her a "damn traitor" ("*maldita traidora*") and a "witch" ("*bruja*").[^cite_note-46] Bukele did not attend an FMLN ethics tribunal on 7 October 2017, saying that the tribunal was biased in favor of Marchelli.[^cite_note-47] On 10 October 2017, he was expelled from the party after the tribunal determined that he had engaged in "defamatory acts" ("*actos difamatorios*") against the party, showed "disrespect" ("*irrespeto*") for women's rights and the party's statutes, and made "disqualifying comments" ("*comentarios descalificadores*") to party members.[^cite_note-48]

Marchelli sued Bukele through the Specialized Investigative Court, but sent a letter to the court in October 2018 saying that she would no longer pursue the matter for health reasons.[^cite_note-49] Despite Marchelli's withdrawal, the FGR proceeded with the case. On 29 March 2019, the Specialized Sentencing Court acquitted Bukele.[^cite_note-50]

In the [2018 legislative and municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Salvadoran_legislative_election), where Bukele was favored to win re-election before his expulsion,[^cite_note-USDW-20] the FMLN had its worst performance [since 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Salvadoran_general_election), the party's first election. It lost six seats in the Legislative Assembly, and 16 municipalities.[^cite_note-51] During the election, Bukele called on his supporters nationwide to [spoil their vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spoilt_vote) or stay home on election day rather than support the FMLN.[^cite_note-52]<sup>: 137 </sup> In February 2019, FMLN presidential communications secretary Roberto Lorenzana stated that Bukele's expulsion was a mistake that cost the party votes.[^cite_note-53] In 2025, Bukele remarked that he was "mistaken" ("*equivocado*") for having previously voted for the FMLN.[^cite_note-54]

### 2019 presidential election

Further information: [2019 Salvadoran presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Salvadoran_presidential_election)

Bukele's popularity as mayor of San Salvador led some journalists to believe that he would run for president in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Salvadoran_presidential_election),[^cite_note-Roque_Baldovinos-18]<sup>: 239 </sup> but he denied that he would.[^cite_note-reordering-29] He eventually expressed interest in running for president with the FMLN, but the party did not consider him as its [vice-presidential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_President_of_El_Salvador) nominee. He wrote on social media that the FMLN had purged him,[^cite_note-Roque_Baldovinos-18]<sup>: 240–241 </sup> and portrayed himself as an [independent politician](2172378.md) who rejected the country's political system.[^cite_note-USDW-20]

On 15 October 2017, Bukele announced his intention to run for president in 2019 and form a new political party.[^cite_note-55] He announced the establishment of the [Nuevas Ideas](61703199.md) party on 25 October 2017 on social media, saying that Nuevas Ideas would seek to remove ARENA and the FMLN from power.[^cite_note-56] During his presidential campaign, Bukele and a network of [YouTubers](57313961.md), bloggers, and [internet trolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troll_\(slang\)) attempted to discredit ARENA and the FMLN.[^cite_note-Roque_Baldovinos-18]<sup>: 242 </sup> Bukele tried to associate the two parties with the governments of previous presidents that were marred by corruption, using slogans such as "There's enough money when nobody steals" and "Return what was stolen".[^cite_note-CNN_FMLN-45] His campaign promises included the creation of an international commission to combat corruption, the development of a trans-national railroad and a new airport, job opportunities for Salvadorans, and reduced crime.[^cite_note-MTC1-57][^cite_note-TG_election-58]

Nayib Bukele and Gabriela Rodríguez posing for press photographers at a campaign rally

Bukele on election day, February 2019

For Bukele to run for president with Nuevas Ideas, he was required to register the party with the [Supreme Electoral Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Electoral_Court_\(El_Salvador\)) (TSE).[^cite_note-59] Although Nuevas Ideas had enough signatures to register,[^cite_note-60] Bukele believed that the TSE would not register the party before the 29 July 2018 presidential nomination deadline.[^cite_note-CIDOB-13][^cite_note-\:0-61]

Bukele registered as a member of [Democratic Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Change_\(El_Salvador\)) and sought the party's presidential nomination before the deadline, but the TSE canceled the party's registration four days before the deadline because Democratic Change failed to receive over 50,000 votes during the 2015 legislative elections. On 29 July 2018, Bukele registered with the [right-wing](56522.md) [Grand Alliance for National Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Alliance_for_National_Unity) (GANA) and received the party's presidential nomination. He selected [Félix Ulloa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Ulloa), a lawyer, as his vice-presidential candidate.[^cite_note-CIDOB-13][^cite_note-\:0-61]

Bukele used social media such as [Facebook](7529378.md), [Instagram](31591547.md), and Twitter extensively throughout his campaign to communicate with his supporters.[^cite_note-AQ1-62] He did not attend either of the two presidential debates, in December 2018 and January 2019, despite saying that he would attend, claiming that the debate rules were not explained to him.[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64] Bukele was the election's front-runner,[^cite_note-AQ1-62] leading virtually every poll by a substantial margin. His three opponents were ARENA's[^cite_note-Calleja_coalition-66] [Carlos Calleja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Calleja), a businessman who owned the [Super Selectos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermercados_Super_Selectos) supermarket chain; the FMLN's former [minister of foreign affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ministers_of_foreign_affairs_of_El_Salvador) [Hugo Martínez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Mart%C3%ADnez_\(politician\)), and [Vamos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vamos_\(El_Salvador\))' Josué Alvarado, a businessman.[^cite_note-MTC1-57][^cite_note-67] On election day, 3 February 2019, Bukele defeated Calleja, Martínez, and Alvarado with 53.1 percent of the vote. He was the first presidential candidate to be elected since [José Napoleón Duarte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Napole%C3%B3n_Duarte) (1984–1989) who was not a member of ARENA or the FMLN.[^cite_note-AQ1-62][^cite_note-68]

## Presidency

### Inaugurations

Nayib Bukele standing at a podium, wearing the Salvadoran presidential sash, and speaking to a crowd

Nayib Bukele and Gabriela Rodríguez de Bukele standing at a balcony and waving to a crowd

Bukele at his first *(top)* and second inaugurations

Bukele's first presidential inauguration was held on 1 June 2019.[^cite_note-Roque_Baldovinos-18]<sup>: 244 </sup> He became the 43rd president of El Salvador as well as the country's youngest president at the age of 37.[^cite_note-69] Bukele held the inauguration ceremony at the [National Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Palace_\(El_Salvador\)) due to its location in Gerardo Barrios Plaza (renovated by Bukele as mayor of San Salvador) instead of in the Blue Room (meeting room) of the Legislative Assembly in an effort to portray his presidency as focusing on the people. Bukele's supporters booed and jeered at the Legislative Assembly deputies as they were introduced.[^cite_note-Roque_Baldovinos-18]<sup>: 244 </sup> He announced a sixteen-person [cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Nayib_Bukele) composed of eight men and eight women.[^cite_note-70]

Bukele's second presidential inauguration was held on 1 June 2024, again at the National Palace.[^cite_note-inaug2-71] During the inauguration, the [Armed Forces of El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_El_Salvador) (FAES) staged a [military parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_parade) as a show of force[^cite_note-EPin-72] and Bukele wore a Napoleonic-cut jacket with gold trim to evoke the image of Venezuelan liberator [Simón Bolívar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar).[^cite_note-73] He described his second inauguration as "the most important moment in our recent history" ("*el momento más importante de nuestra historia reciente*").[^cite_note-EPin-72]

### Crime and prisons

See also: [Crime in El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_El_Salvador)

During Bukele's presidential campaign, he promised to bring an end to gang violence in El Salvador;[^cite_note-TG_election-58] El Salvador was considered one of the world's most dangerous countries due to its gang violence.[^cite_note-74] Most of El Salvador's violent crimes were committed by [MS-13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MS-13) and the [18th Street gang](293711.md) (Barrio 18). Although they are El Salvador's largest gangs, both originated in [Los Angeles](18110.md). MS-13 was formed in the 1980s by Salvadoran refugees fleeing El Salvador's civil war. The 18th Street gang was formed in the 1960s by Mexican immigrants. Much of the gang violence stemmed from income inequality, poverty, poor schools, a lack of job opportunities, and high urbanization.[^cite_note-75]<sup>: 2–3 </sup>

El Salvador's [homicide rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_intentional_homicide_rate) peaked at 107 homicides per 100,000 people in 2015.[^cite_note-76] El Salvador's homicide rate had decreased to 38 homicides per 100,000 people by 2019, still one of the world's highest.[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-B2019-78] Gangs controlled parts of El Salvador,[^cite_note-Roque_Baldovinos-18]<sup>: 237 </sup> and ordered business owners to pay *renta* ([extortion](162449.md)) for [protection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protection_racket) or face violence.[^cite_note-reordering-29] In early 2019, there were an estimated 67,000 gang members in El Salvador.[^cite_note-TG_election-58] During his presidency, Bukele enacted tough-on-crime policies that scholars have characterized as successfully reducing gang activity and violent crime, at the cost of [arbitrary arrest](13435705.md) and alleged widespread human rights abuses.[^cite_note-79]<sup>: 84 </sup>

#### Territorial Control Plan

On 19 June 2019, Bukele announced that his government would implement a seven-phase security [Territorial Control Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_Control_Plan) that sought to disrupt gang finances.[^cite_note-80] The plan began that night at midnight.[^cite_note-81] Phase one, known as "preparation", called for members of the country's security forces — the Armed Forces of El Salvador and the [National Civil Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Civil_Police_\(El_Salvador\)) (PNC) — to be stationed in 12 of the country's 262 municipalities at locations where gangs were known to collect *renta*.[^cite_note-82] The government also implemented a temporary [state of emergency](298608.md) in El Salvador's 28 prisons, putting them on [lockdown](2530301.md) and banning visitors.[^cite_note-83]

Nayib Bukele seated at the head of a table holding a piece of paper in his left hand in front of various cabinet officials

Bukele and members of his cabinet discussing phase two of the Territorial Control Plan, July 2019

Phase two of the plan, known as "Opportunity", began in July 2019 and called for the creation of programs and initiatives to prevent youths predisposed to crime from engaging in criminal activity. The programs and initiatives included creating scholarships, building schools and sports centers, and improving healthcare. Bukele established the Social Fabric Revitalization Unit to implement the phase.[^cite_note-84] Phase three, known as "Modernization", began in August 2019 and called for the improvement of equipment used by the country's security forces; it included issuing new weapons, gear, helicopters, and drones to the security forces.[^cite_note-85] Phase four, known as "Incursion", began in July 2021 when the security forces began patrolling areas with a high gang presence that were considered difficult to access.[^cite_note-86]

Phase five, known as "extraction", began in November 2022. Security forces were ordered to "surround large cities and extract the terrorists \[gang members] who \[were] hiding within the communities, without giving them the slightest possibility of escape".[^cite_note-87] Phase six, known as "integration", began in September 2023, when Bukele established the National Integration Directory to combat poverty and unemployment.[^cite_note-88] Details about phase seven, which has not yet been implemented, are not publicly known.[^cite_note-89]

El Salvador's homicide rate has decreased every year of Bukele's presidency, a downward trend that began in 2016.[^cite_note-FTCECOT-90] According to the Salvadoran government, the country's homicide rate was 38 per 100,000 people in 2019;[^cite_note-B2019-78] 19.7 per 100,000 in 2020;[^cite_note-91] 17.6 per 100,000 in 2021;[^cite_note-92] 7.8 per 100,000 in 2022;[^cite_note-93] 2.4 per 100,000 in 2023;[^cite_note-94] and 1.9 per 100,000 in 2024. Bukele has attributed the decline to his security policies.[^cite_note-2024h-95]

According to [Celia Medrano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celia_Medrano?action=edit\&redlink=1), a human-rights lawyer and former general coordinator of the Commission for the Defense of Human Rights of Central America, it is "impossible" ("*imposible*") to verify the Salvadoran government's homicide figures because there is "no public access" ("*no hay acceso público*") to a daily homicide registry. Medrano stated that deaths in custody are not registered as homicides.[^cite_note-96] Bodies found in mass graves, missing persons, and people killed in police encounters are not included in the government's homicide statistics.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98] In July 2024, then former United States president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) falsely[^cite_note-99] accused Bukele's government of "exporting" criminals to the United States to lower El Salvador's crime rate.[^cite_note-100]

#### Alleged gang negotiations

A screenshot of video surveillance footage showing various men waring face masks inside a prison hallway

Video surveillance footage in a prison, reportedly of [Osiris Luna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osiris_Luna) *(left, 1)* during negotiations with gangs in March 2020

[^cite_note-ISagn-101]

In July 2020, the [International Crisis Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Crisis_Group) (ICG) published an analysis saying that the reason for the decrease in homicides during Bukele's first year in office hypothesized that "quiet, informal understandings" between the government and the gangs. The Salvadoran government denied the ICG's allegations, and the ICG stated that it had no evidence to support the claim.[^cite_note-102]

In September 2020, the Salvadoran digital newspaper *[El Faro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Faro_\(digital_newspaper\))* accused Bukele's government of conducting secret negotiations with MS-13. According to the *El Faro* report, the government agreed to grant MS-13 more freedom in prison in exchange for a reduction in homicides it would commit and support for Nuevas Ideas during the 2021 legislative elections.[^cite_note-103] Bukele denied *El Faro*'s allegations, posting photos on Twitter of gang members rounded up in cramped conditions from an April 2020 prison crackdown.[^cite_note-104]

On 8 December 2021, the [United States Department of the Treasury](53667.md) accused Bukele's government of secretly negotiating with MS-13 and Barrio 18 to lower the country's homicide rate. The department stated that Bukele's government "provided financial incentives" to both gangs to ensure that they would reduce the country's homicide rate and support Nuevas Ideas in the election held earlier that year, echoing *El Faro*'s allegations the year before,[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-AQagn-106] and sanctioned [Osiris Luna Meza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osiris_Luna) (the general director of penal centers and vice-minister of justice) and Social Fabric Revitalization Unit chair Carlos Marroquín Chica for negotiating with the gangs.[^cite_note-ISagn-101]

Bukele denied the department's accusations, saying that the United States sought "absolute submission" from El Salvador rather than cooperation.[^cite_note-AQagn-106] The [United States Department of Justice](52563.md) also accused Bukele's government of releasing gang leaders between 2019 and 2021 as a part of the negotiations, including Élmer "El Crook" Canales Rivera who was released in February 2021 despite having an active [Interpol](14338129.md) arrest warrant against him.[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108]

In June 2025, [ProPublica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ProPublica) reported that U.S. extradition requests of MS-13 leaders considered potential witnesses had been blocked by Bukele's government. The outlet also reported that a U.S. multiagency law enforcement team, Joint Task Force Vulcan, had previously gathered evidence that [United States Agency for International Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Agency_for_International_Development) (USAID) funds to El Salvador had been [laundered](19390.md) and used to pay key MS-13 leaders.[^cite_note-109]

#### Gang crackdown

From 25 to 27 March 2022, gangs in El Salvador committed 87 homicides;[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111] 62 were committed on 26 March alone, the deadliest day in Salvadoran history since the end of the [Salvadoran Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvadoran_Civil_War) (1979–1992).[^cite_note-March_murders_NYT-112] [Florida International University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_International_University) research director José Miguel Cruz attributed the killings to a breakdown in a secret truce between the government and the gangs, a truce that Bukele has denied. Cruz believed that the killings were a message from the gangs to the government for more concessions as a part of the secret truce.[^cite_note-FTcrack-113]

| Avatar of Nayib BukeleAvatar of Nayib Bukele | **Nayib Bukele** (@nayibbukele) tweeted: |
| --- | --- |

> MESSAGE TO THE GANGS: We have 16,000 "homeboys" in our power. Aside from the 1,000 arrested these days. We seized everything they had, even their mattresses, we've rationed their food, and now they won't see the sun. STOP KILLING NOW or they will pay too.

28 March 2022[^cite_note-FTcrack-113][^cite_note-HRWcrack-114][^cite_note-115]

On 27 March 2022, the Legislative Assembly declared a 30-day [state of emergency](298608.md), formally known as a "[state of exception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_exception)" ("*régimen de excepción*")[^cite_note-RdE-116] and sometimes known as the "[war on gangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvadoran_gang_crackdown)".[^cite_note-117] The state of emergency suspended constitutional rights that included [freedom of assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_assembly), [freedom of association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_association), the [right to privacy in communication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_privacy), the right [to be informed of the reason for arrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habeas_corpus), the right to [remain silent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_silence), and the right to [legal representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_counsel). The requirement for any arrested individual to see a judge within 72 hours of arrest was also suspended.[^cite_note-HRWcrack-114]

The military was mobilized in neighborhoods controlled by gangs in an effort to reassert government control,[^cite_note-March_murders_NYT-112] and made large-scale arrests of suspected gang members across the country.[^cite_note-HRWcrack-114] On several occasions, Bukele ordered security forces to blockade certain municipalities to capture all gang members within them. By October 2024, blockades were implemented twice in Apopa,[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] [Cabañas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_the_Caba%C3%B1as_Department),[^cite_note-120] Comasagua,[^cite_note-121] [Nuevo Concepción](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_Nueva_Concepci%C3%B3n),[^cite_note-122] [San Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_San_Marcos),[^cite_note-123] [southern Chalatenango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_southern_Chalatenango),[^cite_note-124] and [Soyapango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_Soyapango).[^cite_note-125]

Soldiers in camouflage uniform, armed with rifles, and standing in an uncovered truck in San Marcos

Soldiers in a truck in [San Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Marcos,_El_Salvador) during the October 2024 [blockade of that city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_of_San_Marcos)

Nayib Bukele surrounded by four government officials while touring through a prison cell block, with prison guards in riot gear are standing in formation in front of cell entrances

Bukele and government officials touring a cell block in the [Terrorism Confinement Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Confinement_Center), March 2022

Bukele has threatened incarcerated gang members. At the beginning of the crackdown, he tweeted that the government had seized incarcerated gang members' belongings, removed their mattresses, and rationed their food.[^cite_note-FTcrack-113][^cite_note-HRWcrack-114] Bukele also posted a video of prisoners sleeping on floors and complaining about a lack of food and sanitation.[^cite_note-PoD-126] He threatened to deprive them of food entirely in April 2022 if the gangs attempted to retaliate against the crackdown, citing rumors about revenge killings.[^cite_note-LATt-127]

After members of Barrio 18 killed three police officers in [Santa Ana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Ana,_El_Salvador) in June 2022, Bukele said at a press conference that the gangs were "going to pay dearly" for the "[ambush](1905.md)" against the police.[^cite_note-128] The government began destroying [gravestones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravestone) belonging to deceased gang members in November 2022 to prevent them from becoming "shrines"[^cite_note-129] and Bukele compared the gravestone destructions to [denazification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denazification) in post-[World War II](32927.md) Germany.[^cite_note-130] He warned Salvadoran parents to keep their children away from gangs, since they would lead to "prison or death".[^cite_note-PoD-126][^cite_note-131]

Shortly after the crackdown began, Bukele called for the construction of a new 20,000-inmate prison.[^cite_note-LATt-127] He announced the construction in July 2022 of the 40,000-inmate [Terrorism Confinement Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Confinement_Center) (CECOT) in [Tecoluca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tecoluca), that would be one of the world's largest prisons.[^cite_note-132] In February 2023, Bukele posted a video on Twitter of him and members of his cabinet touring the prison. It is staffed by 250 police officers and 600 soldiers, and covers 410 acres (170 ha).[^cite_note-133] Bukele posted a video on Twitter on 24 February 2023 of the transfer of the prison's first 2,000 prisoners,[^cite_note-134] and posted a similar video the following month of the transfer of 2,000 more prisoners.[^cite_note-135] By 11 June 2024, CECOT had at least 14,532 inmates.[^cite_note-136] At least 427 people have died in Salvadoran prisons since Bukele's declaration of a state of emergency.[^cite_note-137]

In July 2023, Bukele's government passed a law formalizing the judicial system's existing practice of mass trials by judge, allowing up to 900 people to be convicted in the same trial, without a jury.[^cite_note-138] In March 2026, the Legislative Assembly approved a constitutional amendment to permit [life imprisonment](148681.md) for individuals convicted of murder, rape, or terrorism. Before the amendment was passed, Bukele wrote on social media that "we will see who supports this reform and who will dare to argue that the constitution should continue to prohibit murderers and rapists from remaining in prison".[^cite_note-139]

Liz Throssell, a spokeswoman for the [Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights](22463.md), called the actions of El Salvador's security forces during the gang crackdown an "unnecessary and excessive use of force".[^cite_note-140] [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) stated in May 2022 that there was "mounting evidence" and "credible allegations" that Salvadoran authorities were committing [human rights violations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_violation) such as arbitrary arrests, [enforced disappearances](686148.md), [false confessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_confession), and [deaths in police custody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_in_custody) during the gang crackdown.[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-3319m-142] [Amnesty International](18947898.md) stated the following month that the Salvadoran government had committed "massive human rights violations", including [torture](47702.md), against prisoners.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144]

In June 2024, Bukele told *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* that the security situation in El Salvador had become sustainable and that he and his government hoped to end the state of emergency "in the near future".[^cite_note-145] By 4 March 2025, the state of exception had been extended 36 times by the Legislative Assembly. By that same date, over 85,000 suspected gang members had been arrested,[^cite_note-146] 3,319 of whom were minors according to Human Rights Watch.[^cite_note-3319m-142] The large-scale arrests increased El Salvador's prison population from 37,190 in 2020 to over 105,000 by December 2023. With 1.7 percent of its population in prison, El Salvador has the highest incarceration rate in the world.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148] By November 2024, more than 8,000 people had been released after the government determined that they were innocent.[^cite_note-149] According to human rights organizations, at least 367 people had died in custody by March 2025.[^cite_note-150] A number of opinion polls between May 2022 and June 2023 indicated that 80 to 90 percent of Salvadorans approved of the gang crackdown and measures taken by the government against the gangs.[^cite_note-151]

### Political crises

Main articles: [2020 Salvadoran political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Salvadoran_political_crisis) and [2021 Salvadoran political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Salvadoran_political_crisis)

Soldiers in a room

Members of the Legislative Assembly standing and applauding

The 2020 *(top)* and 2021 crises are known by the [numeronyms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numeronym) "9F" and "1M", respectively.

[^cite_note-EBL-152]

: 77&#x20;

In November 2019, Bukele's administration began trying to secure a \$109 million loan from the [Central American Bank for Economic Integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_American_Bank_for_Economic_Integration) to fund phase three of the Territorial Control Plan.[^cite_note-BBC9F-153] The legislature, which was controlled by ARENA and the FMLN, asked him to give them more time to evaluate the loan.[^cite_note-154] On 6 February 2020, Bukele invoked Article 167 of the [country's constitution](23491231.md) and called for an emergency meeting of the Legislative Assembly to approve the loan. He called for his supporters to rally around the Legislative Assembly during the emergency meeting that was scheduled for 9 February.[^cite_note-155] Although Bukele ordered 40 soldiers into the Legislative Assembly's meeting room on the day of the meeting to coerce legislators into approving the loan,[^cite_note-156] a [quorum](237014.md) was not reached and the loan was not approved.[^cite_note-EP9F-157] Opposition politicians described the crisis as a "[self-coup](1221872.md)",[^cite_note-BBC9F-153][^cite_note-EP9F-157] and it is known in El Salvador as "9F" or "El Bukelazo".[^cite_note-EBL-152]<sup>: 84 </sup>

In the [2021 legislative elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Salvadoran_legislative_election), Nuevas Ideas received a [supermajority](638313.md) in the Legislative Assembly.[^cite_note-158] On 1 May 2021, Nuevas Ideas formed a coalition government with GANA, the [National Coalition Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Coalition_Party_\(El_Salvador\)) (PCN), and the [Christian Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democratic_Party_\(El_Salvador\)) (PDC).[^cite_note-EBL-152]<sup>: 89 </sup> That day, the coalition voted to remove the five justices of the Supreme Court of Justice's constitutional court and Attorney General [Raúl Melara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ra%C3%BAl_Melara).[^cite_note-AJ1M-159] The justices were replaced by five of Bukele's allies, and Melara was replaced by [Rodolfo Delgado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodolfo_Delgado_\(lawyer\)).[^cite_note-160] The purge, known in El Salvador as "1M",[^cite_note-EBL-152]<sup>: 89 </sup> was described by journalists and opposition politicians as a "self-coup" and a "power grab"[^cite_note-AJ1M-159][^cite_note-161][^cite_note-ABC_News-162] and was condemned by the United States.[^cite_note-ABC_News-162][^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164]

### COVID-19 pandemic

Bukele issued an executive decree on 11 March 2020 imposing a "quarantine throughout the national territory" ("*cuarentena en todo el territorio nacional*"), shortly after [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) [director-general](63815422.md) [Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus](39712205.md) declared the [COVID-19](63030231.md) outbreak a [pandemic](62750956.md). The quarantine suspended all school activities for 21 days, prohibited foreigners from entering the country, and mandated a 30-day quarantine for everyone entering the country.[^cite_note-165] Bukele confirmed the first case of [COVID-19 in El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_El_Salvador) on 18 March 2020.[^cite_note-166] The country's first death from the disease was recorded on 31 March.[^cite_note-167] On 5 May 2023, the [Pan American Health Organization](1246706.md) declared the end of the pandemic.[^cite_note-168] According to the WHO, As of 27 September 2023, El Salvador had 201,807 confirmed cases of COVID-19 and 4,230 deaths from the disease by 2 June 2023; the WHO reported that 11,426,688 doses of the [COVID-19 vaccine](63319438.md) had been administered in El Salvador by that date.[^cite_note-169]

A photograph of the entrance of Hospital El Salvador with several Salvadoran flags waving on flag poles in front of the hospital

Entrance of [Hospital El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_El_Salvador)

On 21 March 2020, Bukele imposed a 30-day [nationwide lockdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdowns) in an effort to combat the pandemic. During the lockdown, 4,236 people were arrested by the National Civil Police for violating the lockdown order; 70 were arrested before the lockdown order became public. The arrestees were quarantined in a "containment center". Human rights organizations such as [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) criticized the arrests, citing instances of arbitrary arrests and abuse by police.[^cite_note-170] Amid April 2020 lockdowns in the country's prisons and published images of prisoners lined up in cramped positions, Human Rights Watch called the prisons' living conditions "inhumane" (particularly in light of the pandemic).[^cite_note-171]

On 27 May 2020, the United States donated 250 [ventilators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ventilator) to El Salvador. During a press conference where Bukele received the ventilators, he said that he took [hydroxychloroquine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxychloroquine) as [prophylaxis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preventive_healthcare) and added that "most of the world's leaders use \[hydroxychloroquine] as a prophylaxis".[^cite_note-172] Bukele inaugurated the [Hospital El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_El_Salvador), the largest hospital in Latin America used exclusively for treating cases of COVID-19 at the site of the former [International Center for Fairs and Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Center_for_Fairs_and_Conventions?action=edit\&redlink=1), on 22 June 2020. The hospital had a capacity of 400 beds, 105 intensive-care units, and 295 intermediate-care units staffed by 240 doctors.[^cite_note-173] In August 2020, the hospital's capacity was increased by 575 beds.[^cite_note-174] It began treating conditions other than COVID-19 by June 2022.[^cite_note-175] In April 2021, Bukele inaugurated a vaccination center at Hospital El Salvador to administer up to 10,000 doses of the COVID-19 vaccine per day;[^cite_note-176] the center closed in August 2022, as dose administrations diminished.[^cite_note-177]

Most of El Salvador's COVID-19 vaccines were donated by the United States and China.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] On 13 May 2021, Bukele donated 34,000 doses of the COVID-19 vaccine to seven towns in [Honduras](13394.md) after pleas from their mayors for vaccine doses.[^cite_note-EPexpans-180] El Salvador had received 1.9 million doses at the time, and Honduras had only received 59,000.[^cite_note-181] Gabriel Labrador, a journalist for *El Faro*, told *[El País](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Pa%C3%ADs)* that Bukele made the donation to Honduras to improve his public image in [Central America](6121.md).[^cite_note-EPexpans-180]

### Economy

#### Adoption of bitcoin

See also: [Bitcoin in El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitcoin_in_El_Salvador)

A photograph of an orange Athena Bitcoin cryptocurrency ATM

An Athena Bitcoin ATM in El Zonte

Nayib Bukele and Fernando Romero viewing and standing over a model of the Bitcoin City airport terminal design

Bukele and Mexican architect [Fernando Romero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Romero_\(architect\)) with a model of the planned [Bitcoin City airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport_of_the_Pacific)

Bukele announced at the Bitcoin 2021 conference on 5 June 2021 that he would introduce [a bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitcoin_Law) to the Legislative Assembly that would make [bitcoin](28249265.md) [legal tender](267793.md), saying that it would "generate jobs" and promote "financial inclusion" in the short term.[^cite_note-182] The Legislative Assembly approved the bill three days later.[^cite_note-183] Although the [World Bank](36752.md) rejected a request from the Salvadoran government to assist it with the implementation of bitcoin as legal tender, citing concerns about transparency and the environmental effects of [bitcoin mining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitcoin_mining),[^cite_note-184] Athena Bitcoin announced that it would invest \$1 million to install 1,500 bitcoin [ATMs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automated_teller_machine). This would allow users to exchange U.S. dollars for bitcoin and vice versa.[^cite_note-185]

Bitcoin became legal tender on 7 September 2021 in El Salvador,[^cite_note-EPbtc-186] the first country to do so.[^cite_note-187] It became legal tender alongside the [United States dollar](18717338.md), which had been adopted in 2001 and replaced the [Salvadoran colón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvadoran_col%C3%B3n).[^cite_note-EPbtc-186] About 1,000 people marched in the streets of San Salvador to protest the country's adoption of bitcoin, though other citizens saw the adoption as an "opportunity."[^cite_note-188] According to *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, bitcoin adoption has allowed Salvadorans without bank accounts to "access digital payments, invest savings or boost earnings."[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190]

The day before bitcoin became legal tender, Bukele announced that the Salvadoran government had bought its first 200 bitcoins.[^cite_note-191] Economist [Steve Hanke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Hanke) stated that El Salvador had "the most distressed sovereign debt in the world" due to its adoption of bitcoin,[^cite_note-192] and other economists predicted that the country would likely [default on its debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Default_\(finance\)).[^cite_note-193] As the price of bitcoin rose to \$44,000 in December 2023, Bukele announced that El Salvador's investment into bitcoin had broken even.[^cite_note-194] In March 2024, he stated that El Salvador had made a 50-percent profit from bitcoin. Bukele mocked news-media outlets on Twitter, saying that there were "literally thousands of articles" about El Salvador's bitcoin losses and the same outlets were now "totally silent".[^cite_note-195] By 19 January 2025, the Salvadoran government had 6,043 bitcoins worth \$611.2 million.[^cite_note-196]

In November 2021, Bukele announced that he planned to build [Bitcoin City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bitcoin_City) in the southeastern region of [La Unión](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Uni%C3%B3n_Department) at the base of the [Conchagua volcano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conchagua_\(volcano\)). The city would use [geothermal energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geothermal_energy) to power bitcoin mining.[^cite_note-197] Ricardo Navarro, head of the Salvadoran Center of Appropriate Technology, criticized the plan, adding that it would result in an "environmental disaster".[^cite_note-198] Bukele published images of models of Bitcoin City and its [planned airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport_of_the_Pacific) on Twitter in May 2022, saying that the city would have "no income tax, zero property tax, no procurement tax, zero city tax, and zero CO2 emissions".[^cite_note-199] In December 2023, the Legislative Assembly passed a law that allowed individuals to purchase Salvadoran citizenship by donating bitcoins to El Salvador.[^cite_note-200]

On 18 December 2024, the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) (IMF) agreed to give El Salvador a \$1.4 billion loan in exchange for the Salvadoran government making some concessions from the Bitcoin Law. Concessions included not requiring businesses to accept bitcoin as payment, not accepting bitcoin as tax payments, and reducing the number of bitcoins the government was purchasing.[^cite_note-201] The day after the loan was approved, [Stacy Herbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stacy_Herbert), the director of the National Bitcoin Office, stated that El Salvador would continue to buy bitcoins at an "accelerated rate" ("*ritmo acelerado*")[^cite_note-202] and the government purchased 11 bitcoins then worth over \$1 million in total.[^cite_note-203] On 29 January 2025, the Salvadoran government amended the Bitcoin Law to remove bitcoin's status as legal tender and currency but still allows its use as payment.[^cite_note-204]

In March 2025, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* wrote that El Salvador's bitcoin experiment had been a failure, bringing more costs than benefits to the Salvadoran economy.[^cite_note-205]

In 2025, the Salvadoran economy strengthened to 3.9% GDP growth from 2.6 the previous year with private investment growing 36%.[^cite_note-206]

#### Economic Plan

During Bukele's second inauguration, he stated that his second term would focus on improving the Salvadoran economy with "bitter medicine" ("*medicina amarga*").[^cite_note-EFep-207][^cite_note-208] In July 2024, Bukele threatened to mass-arrest vendors, importers, and distributors who engaged in [price gouging](879011.md).[^cite_note-EFep-207][^cite_note-209] Later that month, he announced the beginning of a six-phase Economic Plan ("*Plan Económico*"). Phase one, known as "Feeding" ("*Alimentación*"), involved the establishment of 30 food distribution centers and the removal of tariffs on certain agricultural imports for ten years.[^cite_note-EFep-207][^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211] Phase two, known as "Technology" ("*Tecnología*"), involved the construction of [data centers](579730.md) and [technological parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technological_park) in El Salvador. Bukele stated that his Economic Plan would create 4,000 jobs.[^cite_note-EFep-207] Phase three, known as "Logistics" ("*Logística*"), involved the investment of US\$1.6 billion into modernizing the ports of [Acajutla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acajutla) and [La Unión](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Uni%C3%B3n,_El_Salvador) and the Turkish company Yılport Holdings operating the ports for 50 years.[^cite_note-212]

Nayib Bukele with Gisela Sánchez

Bukele with Gisela Sánchez, the president of the [Central American Bank for Economic Integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_American_Bank_for_Economic_Integration)

On 15 September 2024, Bukele stated that his 2025 government budget would not include "a single cent of debt for current spending" ("*solo centavo de deuda para gasto corriente*") and that his government would not take out foreign loans to pay for the budget.[^cite_note-213] On 16 October, El Salvador and [J.P. Morgan & Co.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J.P._Morgan_&_Co.) agreed to restructure US\$1.03 billion of the country's debt as a part of a [debt-for-nature swap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debt-for-nature_swap), which Bukele described as "reaffirm\[ing] this government's commitment to economic growth".[^cite_note-214] In the agreement, El Salvador would allocate US\$352 million in savings towards conserving the environment around the [Lempa River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lempa_River).[^cite_note-215] On three occasions in 2024, Bukele offered to buy back billions of dollars worth of [government bonds](60737.md) due by 2034.[^cite_note-216]

In November 2024, the Central American Bank for Economic Integration announced that it would give the Salvadoran government \$646 million to finance infrastructure projects. Bukele stated that the bank's support would help El Salvador's "economic takeoff" ("*despegue económico*").[^cite_note-217] Later that month, Bukele wrote on X that he supported mining gold, describing it as "wealth that could transform El Salvador". He further described the country's metal mining ban as "absurd".[^cite_note-218] The Catholic Church, which supported the mining ban's implementation in 2017, called on Bukele to not repeal the ban, citing environmental concerns.[^cite_note-219] The Legislative Assembly repealed the ban on 23 December 2024[^cite_note-220] and Bukele approved the law that same day.[^cite_note-221] In 2025, at least 2,500 unlicensed [street vendors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawker_\(trade\)) were evicted from downtown San Salvador as part of Bukele's economic revitalization plan.[^cite_note-222]

### Foreign policy

See also: [List of international trips made by Nayib Bukele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_trips_made_by_Nayib_Bukele)

Bukele stated in June 2019 that his government would no longer recognize [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md) as the [president of Venezuela](3329692.md), instead recognizing [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3) as Venezuela's legitimate president during [Venezuela's presidential crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_presidential_crisis). On 3 November of that year, he expelled Maduro-appointed Venezuelan diplomats from El Salvador.[^cite_note-223] Bukele considers Maduro to be a [dictator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictator).[^cite_note-224] Bukele rejected the results of the [2024 Venezuelan presidential election](60825396.md) as a "fraud" ("*fraude*") and stated that he would not restore relations with Venezuela unless there were "real elections" ("*elecciones de verdad*").[^cite_note-225]

Bukele refused to recognize the presidency of [Manuel Merino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Merino) in Peru in November 2020, calling Merino's government "putschist" ("*golpista*").[^cite_note-226] He and the Legislative Assembly denounced the results of the [2021 Nicaraguan general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Nicaraguan_general_election), that were seen by several governments as fraudulent.[^cite_note-FAL-227] El Salvador has abstained from resolutions critical of Nicaragua at the Organization of American States since 2022, with Bukele's government citing "[non-interference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-interventionism)" ("*no injerencia*") as justification.[^cite_note-228] In 2024, El Salvador was the only country to abstain on an OAS resolution to condemn Ecuador for [raiding the Mexican embassy in Quito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_raid_on_the_Mexican_embassy_in_Ecuador) to arrest former Ecuadorian vice president [Jorge Glas](38822595.md).[^cite_note-229]

A photograph of Nayib Bukele with several North and South American leaders at the Shield of the Americas summit

Bukele at the [Shield of the Americas](82587150.md) summit in March 2026

In February 2022, Bukele accused United States president [Joe Biden](145422.md) of "[crying wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Boy_Who_Cried_Wolf)" about a [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-230] Bukele did not comment on the invasion when it began later that month, posting instead on Twitter about bitcoin and bonds.[^cite_note-231] Throughout 2022, El Salvador abstained from votes on United Nations resolutions condemning the Russian invasion of Ukraine.[^cite_note-232] Bukele condemned the [7 October attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_October_attacks), describing [Hamas](13913.md) as "savage beasts" who "do not represent the [Palestinians](23267.md)", comparing the group to MS-13. He tweeted that "the best thing that could happen to the Palestinian people is for Hamas to completely disappear".[^cite_note-233]

In March 2024, Bukele offered to send a mission to Haiti to "fix" the [country's gang war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gang_war_in_Haiti) with [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) support.[^cite_note-234] In October 2024, El Salvador agreed to provide soldiers to conduct street patrols and aerial surveillance for the [Multinational Security Support Mission in Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_Security_Support_Mission_in_Haiti),[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236] and the first Salvadoran soldiers arrived in [Port-au-Prince](57391.md) on 3 January 2025.[^cite_note-237] In March 2026, Bukele attended the [Shield of the Americas](82587150.md) summit in [Miami](53846.md) along with several other Latin American leaders.[^cite_note-238]

#### Relations with the United States

See also: [El Salvador–United States relations](15982838.md)

Nayib Bukele and Marco Rubio talking on a balcony overssing Lake Coatepeque

Bukele and U.S. Secretary of State [Marco Rubio](5502549.md) in February 2025

During Bukele's September 2019 meeting with Trump, Bukele called on Trump to promote legal migration in an effort to combat [illegal immigration](5044573.md) and to maintain the United States' [temporary protected status](20497716.md) (TPS) policy for [Salvadorans](12971440.md) living in the United States.[^cite_note-239] The following month, Bukele confirmed that the United States would continue TPS for Salvadorans.[^cite_note-240] In February 2021, Biden refused to meet Bukele when he arrived unannounced in Washington, D.C. to meet him.[^cite_note-241] Bukele did not attend the [9th Summit of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_Summit_of_the_Americas) in June 2022 due to frustration with the U.S. government's allegations of corruption and human rights abuses by his government.[^cite_note-242]

Some [Democratic Party](5043544.md) members have been critical of Bukele's government, and members of the [Republican Party](32070.md) have supported him and his policies.[^cite_note-243] Bukele and [Norma Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norma_Torres), a member of the U.S. Congress representing [California's 35th congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California's_35th_congressional_district), engaged in an April 2021 argument on Twitter about illegal immigration at the United States' southern border.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] Torres accused Bukele in November 2022 of interfering in that month's [35th congressional district election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections_in_California#District_35) by endorsing Republican challenger Mike Cargile.[^cite_note-246] In January 2024, fourteen Democratic members of Congress sent a letter to Biden about Bukele's "authoritarian" actions.[^cite_note-247] Meanwhile, Republican congressmen such as [Tom Cotton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Cotton), [Matt Gaetz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Gaetz), and [Marco Rubio](5502549.md) have praised Bukele's policies on crime.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249]

The [second Trump administration](78298108.md) considers Bukele to be an important ally in mitigating immigration to the U.S. from Central America.[^cite_note-250] In February 2025, Bukele offered Secretary of State Rubio to accept non-Salvadoran [deportees from the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_deportation_of_illegal_immigrants_in_the_second_presidency_of_Donald_Trump), including convicted American prisoners "of U.S. citizenship and legal residents".[^cite_note-Hudson-251] Rubio described the offer as the "most unprecedented and extraordinary migratory agreement anywhere in the world".[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253] Bukele stated that these "dangerous American criminals" would be incarcerated in CECOT.[^cite_note-Hudson-251] In March 2025, the United States [deported 238 alleged members](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_2025_Venezuelan_deportations) of the Venezuelan gang [Tren de Aragua](70859925.md) to El Salvador.[^cite_note-254] Bukele met Trump in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office) in April 2025 where they discussed immigration. Both Trump and Bukele stated they could not unilaterally release [Kilmar Abrego Garcia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_Kilmar_Abrego_Garcia), a migrant deported due to an administrative error.[^cite_note-255]

#### Relations with China

See also: [China–El Salvador relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93El_Salvador_relations)

Nayib Bukele and standing at a podium in front of Chinese and Salvadoran flags with a construction crew in the background

Bukele with Chinese ambassador Zhang Yanhui at the beginning of construction of the [National Stadium of El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estadio_Nacional_de_El_Salvador) in November 2023

In 2018, El Salvador cut diplomatic ties with Taiwan and recognized the People's Republic of China as China's legitimate government. This led to Bukele and the United States accusing China of interfering in Salvadoran and Latin American politics. Despite Bukele's criticism of China before becoming president, Vice President Félix Ulloa stated in May 2019 that Bukele's government would not restore [diplomatic relations with Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Salvador%E2%80%93Taiwan_relations).[^cite_note-china-256] In December 2019, Bukele met [Chinese leader](262766.md) [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) in [Beijing](18603746.md)[^cite_note-257] and signed a "gigantic" infrastructure agreement with China for an unknown amount of money.[^cite_note-china-256] El Salvador and China have cooperated on infrastructure projects in El Salvador such as the [National Library of El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_El_Salvador) (completed in November 2023)[^cite_note-258] and the [National Stadium of El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estadio_Nacional_de_El_Salvador) (construction began in November 2023).[^cite_note-259]

In November 2022, Bukele announced that El Salvador and China had begun negotiations for a [free trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade) agreement between the countries. China donated fertilizer and wheat flour to El Salvador and, according to a Salvadoran government official, offered to buy El Salvador's external bond debt. Bukele stated that a free trade agreement with China was "very important" because El Salvador had been "isolated from \[the] potential" of China's economic strength.[^cite_note-260]

### Alleged governmental corruption

See also: [Corruption in El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_El_Salvador)

Twenty of Bukele's governmental institutions were investigated by the office of the attorney general in November 2020 for corruption related to the COVID-19 pandemic,[^cite_note-261] but the investigations were halted after the attorney general was removed by the Legislative Assembly on 1 May 2021.[^cite_note-262] The United States has placed sanctions on several of Bukele's government officials, labeling them as corrupt. The officials include Javier Argueta (presidential advisor), Osiris Luna Meza (general director of penal centers), Carlos Marroquín Chica (chairman of the Social Fabric Reconstruction Unit), Martha Carolina Recinos (chief of the cabinet), Rogelio Rivas (former minister of justice), Ernesto Sanabria (press secretary), and [Alejandro Zelaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Zelaya) (former minister of finance).[^cite_note-WOLAengel-263][^cite_note-absurd-264][^cite_note-265] The U.S. also considered some of Bukele's Legislative Assembly allies corrupt, including [Guillermo Gallegos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_Gallegos) and Christian Guevara.[^cite_note-WOLAengel-263][^cite_note-266] Some of the individuals are included on the U.S. State Department's "Engel List" of Central American politicians and judges considered "corrupt and undemocratic".[^cite_note-267] Bukele called the sanctions and labels "absurd".[^cite_note-absurd-264] In May 2021, the United States diverted El Salvador funding from government institutions to civil society groups to combat perceived corruption in Bukele's government.[^cite_note-268]

On 11 November 2021, Bukele introduced the "Foreign Agents Law" to the Legislative Assembly with the goal of "prohibiting foreign interference" in Salvadoran political affairs.[^cite_note-FAL-227] According to Minister of the Interior [Juan Carlos Bidegain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Carlos_Bidegain_Hanan%C3%ADa), the law was meant to "guarantee the security, national sovereignty and social and political stability of the country".[^cite_note-FAL-227] Although Bukele stated that the law was modeled on the United States' [Foreign Agents Registration Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Agents_Registration_Act) (FARA), critics have compared it to Nicaraguan laws that institute [press censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship) by shutting down organizations and arresting journalists.[^cite_note-FAL-227] [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) reported on 16 December 2021 that 91 Twitter accounts belonging to journalists, lawyers, and activists were blocked by Bukele and governmental institutions.[^cite_note-blocked-269] In October 2024, when investigative journalists published a report that found that Bukele, his three brothers, wife, and mother had purchased 34 properties valued at US\$9 million during Bukele's first presidential term, Bukele referred to the journalists as "imbeciles" and denied accusations of corruption.[^cite_note-270]

### Anti-corruption campaigns

Bukele established the [International Commission Against Impunity in El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Commission_Against_Impunity_in_El_Salvador?action=edit\&redlink=1) (CICIES) in September 2019, an [anti-corruption](58111406.md) commission to combat drug trafficking, corruption, and [white-collar crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-collar_crime). CICIES was operated by the Salvadoran government and the [Organization of American States](22788.md) (OAS), and cooperated with the National Civil Police to form an anti-corruption unit.[^cite_note-271] Bukele dissolved CICIES in June 2021 after the OAS named [Ernesto Muyshondt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernesto_Muyshondt) an anti-corruption advisor;[^cite_note-272] Ernesto Muyshondt was accused by the Salvadoran government of electoral fraud and illegal negotiation with gang members to vote for ARENA during the [2014 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Salvadoran_presidential_election). He was arrested[^cite_note-273] and was scheduled to go on trial in April 2024, despite concerns about his health.[^cite_note-274]

On 1 June 2023, during a speech celebrating his fourth year in office, Bukele stated that his government would begin a "war against corruption" ("*guerra contra la corrupción*").[^cite_note-EPwac-275] He announced that he would build a prison for individuals convicted of white-collar crimes that would be similar to the Terrorism Confinement Center.[^cite_note-276] Bukele stated that the police and military would arrest white-collar criminals like they capture gang members in the gang crackdown.[^cite_note-EPwac-275] He added that Attorney General [Rodolfo Delgado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodolfo_Delgado_\(lawyer\)) was in the process of raiding and confiscating assets worth up to \$68 million from former Salvadoran president [Alfredo Cristiani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_Cristiani) as part of the anti-corruption campaign.[^cite_note-277] Others charged during Bukele's war on corruption include deputies Erick García,[^cite_note-278] [Lorena Peña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorena_Pe%C3%B1a)[^cite_note-279] and [Alberto Romero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Armando_Romero_Rodr%C3%ADguez),[^cite_note-280] and national security advisor [Alejandro Muyshondt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Muyshondt).[^cite_note-281]

In 2022, the last full year before the war against corruption was announced, [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International)'s [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index) gave El Salvador a score of 33 out of 100 and ranked it 116th out of 180 countries.[^cite_note-282] According to a February 2023 CID-Gallup opinion poll, only four percent of Salvadorans considered corruption the country's most pressing issue.[^cite_note-283]

### Municipal and legislative reductions

Nayib Bukele standing at a podium and holding up a legislative proposal in his right hand

Bukele holding a bill proposing the reduction of the number of municipalities from 262 to 44

In December 2022, Bukele tweeted that he believed that the country's [262 municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_El_Salvador) should be reduced to 50. He called it "absurd" ("*absurdo*") that El Salvador, around 8,100 square miles (21,000 km<sup>2</sup>) in size, had so many municipalities.[^cite_note-284] Some lawyers and politicians criticized Bukele's proposed reduction as an attempt to consolidate power by [gerrymandering](12987.md).[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286]<sup>: 20 </sup> His allies supported the proposal, with some proposing a reduction in the number of Legislative Assembly seats.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289]

On 1 June 2023, during a speech commemorating his fourth year in office, Bukele announced that he would present two proposals to the Legislative Assembly.
