One sought to reduce the number of seats in the assembly from 84 to 60, and the other sought to reduce the number of municipalities from 262 to 44.[^cite_note-262m-290] Bukele justified the legislative reduction by saying that the legislature had 60 seats before the signing of the [Chapultepec Peace Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapultepec_Peace_Accords) in 1992 that ended the Salvadoran Civil War, and the accords' only accomplishment was the addition of 24 seats to the legislature.[^cite_note-291] About the municipal-reduction proposal, he stated that the 262 municipalities would retain their cultural identities and be classified as districts.[^cite_note-262m-290] The Legislative Assembly approved the proposal for legislative reductions on 7 June 2023,[^cite_note-292] and approved the proposal for municipal reductions six days later.[^cite_note-effect-293] Both reductions became effective on 1 May 2024.[^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295]

### Re-election campaigns

Further information: [2024 Salvadoran presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Salvadoran_presidential_election) and [2027 Salvadoran presidential election](80593140.md)

On 3 September 2021, the Supreme Court of Justice ruled that the president can serve two consecutive terms in office. The ruling overturned a 2014 ruling that presidents had to wait ten years to be eligible to run for re-election. Constitutional lawyers criticized the ruling, saying that consecutive re-election violates [El Salvador's constitution](23491231.md).[^cite_note-reelection_AJ-296][^cite_note-reelection_AP-297] The 2021 ruling allowed Bukele to run for re-election in the [2024 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Salvadoran_presidential_election). ARENA and the FMLN protested the court's ruling, with an ARENA spokesperson calling it a "precursor to a dictatorship" and an FMLN representative saying that the state is serving only one person: Bukele.[^cite_note-reelection_AP-297] The ruling was also condemned by the United States government. [Jean Elizabeth Manes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Elizabeth_Manes), chargé d'affaires of the United States to El Salvador, called it "clearly contrary to the Salvadoran constitution".[^cite_note-reelection_AP-297] According to Manes, the ruling was a direct result of the May 2021 legislative replacement of the Supreme Court justices.[^cite_note-298]

| video icon [Bukele's speech celebrating 201 years independence, where he announced he would run for re-election in 2024](https://twitter.com/nayibbukele/status/1570593844202291200) (34\:00) |

On 15 September 2022, during a speech commemorating El Salvador's 201st anniversary of independence, Bukele announced that he would run for re-election in 2024. According to Bukele, "developed countries have re-election, and thanks to the new configuration of the democratic institution of our country, now El Salvador will too".[^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301] Constitutional lawyers criticized his announcement, saying that presidential re-election violates "at least" four articles of the El Salvador constitution.[^cite_note-LATNB-302]

Bukele, Félix Ulloa and two other men

Bukele registering his 2024 presidential candidacy with the TSE

Bukele registered as a presidential pre-candidate on 26 June 2023 with Nuevas Ideas; Ulloa registered as Bukele's vice-presidential pre-candidate.[^cite_note-303] Nuevas Ideas nominated Bukele and Ulloa as their presidential and vice-presidential candidates on 9 July.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305] The party began registering Bukele and Ulloa's candidacies with the [TSE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Electoral_Court_\(El_Salvador\)) on 26 October, the last day to do so.[^cite_note-306] On 3 November 2023, the TSE registered their candidacies[^cite_note-307] amidst opposition requests to reject Bukele's candidacy.[^cite_note-308]

On 30 November 2023, the Legislative Assembly granted Bukele and Ulloa leaves of absence to focus on their re-election campaign. The leave went into effect the following day and Bukele's presidential powers were suspended.[^cite_note-309] The Legislative Assembly named [Claudia Rodríguez de Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudia_Rodr%C3%ADguez_de_Guevara), Bukele's presidential secretary, as the presidential designate; Rodríguez was the first woman in Salvadoran history to hold presidential power.[^cite_note-310] Her appointment was criticized by some lawyers and opposition politicians as unconstitutional.[^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313]

Including Bukele, there were six presidential candidates in the 2024 election.[^cite_note-314] His primary opponents were ARENA's Joel Sánchez, a businessman, and the FMLN's [Manuel Flores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Flores_\(Salvadoran_politician\)), a former legislator.[^cite_note-MTC2024-315] Bukele led Sánchez and Flores by large margins in [opinion polling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_for_the_2024_Salvadoran_general_election) before the election.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317] Bukele promised to maintain the gang crackdown, invest in infrastructure projects, and promote economic growth during his second term.[^cite_note-MTC2024-315] On 4 February 2024, he won re-election with 84.65 percent of the vote.[^cite_note-EM_2024-318] Bukele was the first Salvadoran president to be re-elected since [Maximiliano Hernández Martínez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximiliano_Hern%C3%A1ndez_Mart%C3%ADnez) in [1944](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1944_Salvadoran_presidential_election).[^cite_note-319] Nuevas Ideas retained its Legislative Assembly supermajority and, with its allies, won 43 of the country's 44 municipalities.[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321] Several news outlets described the election results as a "[landslide victory](41053306.md)" for Bukele and Nuevas Ideas,[^cite_note-322] and Bukele described his victory as "the record in the entire democratic history of the world".[^cite_note-323] The TSE granted Bukele his presidential credentials on 29 February[^cite_note-324] and his second term began on 1 June.[^cite_note-inaug2-71]

In a 2024 interview with *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))*, Bukele declined to run for a third term in the [next presidential election](80593140.md).[^cite_note-325] In July 2025, the Legislative Assembly passed several constitutional amendments, among them, the abolition of presidential term limits that made Bukele eligible to run for re-election indefinitely. Additionally, the legislature voted to move the date of the next presidential election from 2029 to 2027, reducing the length of Bukele's second term by two years.[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327] In December 2025, Bukele told YouTuber [TheGrefg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TheGrefg) that "if it were up to me, I would stay for 10 more years".[^cite_note-328] On 28 June 2026, Bukele registered as a pre-candidate with Nuevas Ideas, seeking a third presidential term;[^cite_note-329] on 12 July, the party formally nominated him as its presidential candidate.[^cite_note-2027nom-330]

## Personal life

### Family

Bukele and his wife walking with their two daughters surrounded by soldiers

Bukele with his wife and their two daughters in 2024

Bukele began dating psychologist and former ballet dancer [Gabriela Rodríguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriela_Rodr%C3%ADguez_de_Bukele) in 2004[^cite_note-331] and the two married on 6 December 2014.[^cite_note-Revista_Elementos-10][^cite_note-Bernal-16] The couple have two daughters. Their first was born in 2019[^cite_note-332] and their second in 2023.[^cite_note-333]

### Wealth

According to the Salvadoran government's transparency website, Bukele's monthly presidential salary was \$5,181 in July 2019. According to the website, he had a net worth of \$2,548,967 at that time.[^cite_note-334] Bukele acquired most of his wealth through business ventures before entering politics.[^cite_note-335] Bukele owns a coffee farm. In July 2024, he began donating coffee beans grown on his farm to local businesses[^cite_note-336] and launched the Bean of Fire coffee brand.[^cite_note-337] Through Bean of Fire, Bukele became one of El Salvador's largest coffee producers. In 2024, the *Redacción* magazine published the second of its two investigations against him, which found that his family land holding increased 12-fold during his first five years as president and that his family's wealth had increased considerably.[^cite_note-338]

### Religion

Bukele's religious beliefs were controversial during his 2019 presidential campaign, with rumors that he was a [Christian](18337522.md), a [Muslim](19541.md), or an [atheist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism).[^cite_note-RAP-339] The controversy began when pictures from 2011 of Bukele praying at a mosque with his father and brothers spread on social media.[^cite_note-340][^cite_note-EMmez-341] Bukele dismissed the controversy as an attempt by the political right to exploit [Islamophobia](162173.md) in the predominantly Catholic country.[^cite_note-RAP-339]

Although Bukele does not identify with any religion, he has stated that he believes in [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God) and [Jesus](1095706.md).[^cite_note-CIDOB-13][^cite_note-EMmez-341] In a 2015 interview, Bukele said: "I am not a person who believes much in the liturgy of religions. However, I believe in God, in Jesus Christ. I believe in his word, I believe in his word revealed in the [Holy Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible). And I know that God does not reject anyone because of their origins".[^cite_note-TTOI-11] Before that year, some Salvadorans believed that he was a Muslim.[^cite_note-Aguilar_Vasquez-342]<sup>: 166 </sup> Bukele has referred to [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) verses,[^cite_note-RAP-339] God, and Saint [Óscar Romero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%93scar_Romero) — the [archbishop of San Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_San_Salvador) who was assassinated in 1980 — in some of his speeches,[^cite_note-Aguilar_Vasquez-342]<sup>: 166–172 </sup> and has called himself an "instrument of God" ("*instrumento de Dios*").[^cite_note-Aguilar_Vasquez-342]<sup>: 177 </sup> He met with [Pope Francis](1687680.md) in April 2015, saying that the pope told him that [Rutilio Grande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rutilio_Grande) — a Jesuit priest who was assassinated in 1977 — would soon be beatified.[^cite_note-Aguilar_Vasquez-342]<sup>: 166 </sup>

## Political views

Nayib Bukele and Donald Trump standing side-by-side

Bukele with [U.S. president](24113.md) [Donald Trump](4848272.md) (to whom Bukele and his governing style have been compared) in April 2025

Nayib Bukele and Javier Milei standing in front of the flags of El Salvador and Argentina

Bukele with [Argentine president](229473.md) [Javier Milei](56599840.md) (to whom Bukele and his policies have been compared) in September 2024

As mayor of Nuevo Cuscatlán, Bukele described himself as part of the "[radical left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-left_politics)"[^cite_note-Bergengruen-343] because he wanted "radical changes" ("*cambios radicales*") for El Salvador;[^cite_note-344] he also stated that his family had always had significant connections with the Salvadoran political left. Bukele believed in [social justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_justice) and the state obligation to guarantee Salvadorans the opportunity for "health, education, \[and] productive infrastructure" ("*salud, educación, \[e] infraestructura productiva*"). Some FMLN members criticized Bukele's work as a businessman, believing that it contradicted the "historic goal of the proletariat" ("*papel histórico del proletariado*"): eliminating capitalism.[^cite_note-Bernal-16]

Since becoming president, Bukele has stated that he does not adhere to any specific political ideology. He has criticized the political left and right in El Salvador for dividing the country after the civil war.[^cite_note-popularity-345] In an interview with *Time*'s Vera Bergengruen, he stated that he did not consider himself either left- or right-wing.[^cite_note-Bergengruen-343] *El Faro*'s editorial board has described Bukele as a "politician without an ideology" ("*político sin ideología*").[^cite_note-346] Despite Bukele's ostensible ideologic neutrality, some journalists and political analysts have described him as a [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populist),[^cite_note-APpop-347][^cite_note-APwcd-348][^cite_note-349] a [right-wing populist](8354382.md),[^cite_note-EBL-152]<sup>: 82 </sup>[^cite_note-Pereira-350]<sup>: 5 </sup>[^cite_note-351] and a [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative).[^cite_note-352][^cite_note-353] Bukele himself has received support from conservatives abroad in both Latin America[^cite_note-358] and the United States,[^cite_note-362] particularly for his anti-crime policies, as well as criticism for [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding) and consolidating power.[^cite_note-Kurylo-363] His political views and government policies have been referred to by some journalists as "Bukelism"[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] or the "Bukele method".[^cite_note-Kurylo-363][^cite_note-CNNtbm-367][^cite_note-368] Bukelism is also seen by some as a [Third Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Way) ideology.[^cite_note-dup-0-2-369][^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371]

Some [Western](21208200.md) journalists have compared Bukele to U.S. President [Donald Trump](4848272.md), citing Bukele's style of governance, government policies, rhetoric, and criticism of the press as similar to Trump's.[^cite_note-APpop-347][^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374] In 2019, *[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy)*'s Melissa Vida referred to Bukele as "El Salvador's Trump"[^cite_note-375] and *[Jacobin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobin_\(magazine\))*'s Hilary Goodfriend called him "the Donald Trump of Central America".[^cite_note-376] Bukele was one of the first world leaders to congratulate Trump after his victory in the [2024 United States presidential election](59568546.md).[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] In 2024, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* described Bukele as politically "hard right" and compared his policies and ideological views to Argentinian president [Javier Milei](56599840.md), former Brazilian president [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md), and now-president of Chile [José Antonio Kast](55052190.md).[^cite_note-BMKB-379]

Bukele is a [critic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Soros_conspiracy_theories) of [George Soros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Soros),[^cite_note-380] saying in May 2023 that "in all the countries of Latin America, there are outlets and 'journalists' paid by Soros" ("*en todos los países de Latinoamérica hay medios y 'periodistas' pagados por Soros*").[^cite_note-381] In February 2024, Bukele spoke at the American [Conservative Political Action Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Political_Action_Conference) (CPAC) and accused Soros of attempting to "dictate public politics and laws" ("*dictar políticas públicas y leyes*") in El Salvador.[^cite_note-382] Bukele also expressed opposition to [globalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalism), saying that "it's already dead" in El Salvador.[^cite_note-BMKB-379]

### Social issues

In 2023, Celia Medrano described Bukele's positions on social issues as "flexible" ("*flexibles*") and a "liquid ideology" ("*ideología líquida*"). She explained that Bukele changes his positions to appease as many voters as possible and to gauge public opinion on issues such as [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_of_same-sex_unions_in_El_Salvador) and [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_El_Salvador).[^cite_note-EP_abortion-383]

Bukele stated in 2014 that he was an ally of the [LGBT community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_community), supported their [civil rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_El_Salvador), and opposed discrimination against LGBT individuals.[^cite_note-Aguilar_Vasquez-342]<sup>: 167 </sup> In August 2021, Bukele proposed constitutional reform to legalize same-sex marriage in El Salvador. The proposal would have changed text in the constitution that defined marriage as being between "a man and a woman" ("*hombre y mujer*") to defining marriage as between "spouses" ("*cónyuges*"), and would have prohibited discrimination based on [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation). The earliest Bukele's proposal could have gone into effect would have been 2027, since it would have to be approved by two consecutive sessions of the Legislative Assembly.[^cite_note-Observador-384] Bukele stated the following month that the proposed constitutional reform would not legalize same-sex marriage, posting on Facebook that the original text would remain intact.[^cite_note-Reuters_social-385] In March 2024, Bukele stated that his government would remove "all traces" of "[gender ideologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_studies) in schools and colleges".[^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387] In June 2024, Bukele fired 300 bureaucrats from the ministry of culture for promoting policies that were "incompatible" with his emphasis on "patriotic and family values".[^cite_note-388]

El Salvador has one of the world's strictest abortion laws, banning it in all circumstances with no exceptions.[^cite_note-389] In 2013, when a Salvadoran woman known as "Beatriz" was denied an abortion despite doctors saying that she would die in childbirth, Bukele called those who denied her an abortion "fanatics" ("*fanáticos*"). Bukele stated in October 2018 that he only supported abortion in cases where the mother's life was at risk, and expressed opposition to abortion on demand. Shortly after becoming president, he opposed abortion under any circumstances. In an interview with Puerto Rican rapper [Residente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Residente), Bukele said that "someday, we are going to recognize that \[abortion] is a great [genocide](12441.md)" ("*algún día, nos vamos a dar cuenta de que es un gran genocidio*").[^cite_note-EP_abortion-383] Bukele's August 2021 constitutional-reform proposal considered legalizing abortion in cases where the mother's life was at risk, adding that the proposal would have recognized the right to life for mother and child.[^cite_note-Observador-384] He changed his mind the following month, saying that abortion would not be decriminalized and recognizing the "RIGHT TO LIFE (from the moment of conception)" of the unborn. Bukele also ruled out [euthanasia](9587.md).[^cite_note-Reuters_social-385]

### Central American unity

| Avatar of Nayib BukeleAvatar of Nayib Bukele | **Nayib Bukele** (@nayibbukele) tweeted: |
| --- | --- |

[Spanish](26825.md):&#x20;

> Aunque por ahora suena a utopía, el sentido común debería apuntar a la unificación de Centroamérica en un solo país.

---

Translation:&#x20;

> &#x20;Although for now it sounds like a [utopia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utopia), common sense should point to the unification of Central America into a single country.&#x20;

27 January 2017[^cite_note-390]

Bukele is a proponent of [Central American reunification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_American_reunification), an ideology that calls for Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, and Nicaragua to reestablish the [Federal Republic of Central America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Republic_of_Central_America), and has stated that Central America should be "one single nation" in some of his speeches.[^cite_note-EPexpans-180][^cite_note-391] In January 2024, he reaffirmed on Twitter that he believes that Central America should unite as a single country; each individual country is small and lacks natural resources, but a unified Central American population and biodiversity would help strengthen the region. In his tweet, Bukele conceded that he needed "the will of the peoples" ("*la voluntad de los pueblos*") of Central America to unite the region.[^cite_note-392]

Bukele was the president *[pro tempore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro_tempore)* of the [Central American Integration System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_American_Integration_System) (SICA), an economic and political organization, from 5 June to 22 December 2019.[^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395] In February 2020, Bukele signed an agreement with the Guatemalan government to remove restrictions on border crossings between El Salvador and Guatemala and designate flights between the countries as "domestic" flights to promote tourism. The agreement gave Bukele's government the ability to build a port on the [Caribbean Sea](7822.md) in Guatemalan territory, that would give El Salvador access to the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md). He described the agreement as "the greatest step to the integration of Central America in the last 180 years" ("*el mayor paso en la integración de Centroamérica en los últimos 180 años*").[^cite_note-396]

According to Will Freeman of the [Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations), Bukele has styled himself as the "second coming of [Francisco Morazán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Moraz%C3%A1n)", a Honduran politician who was [president of the Federal Republic of Central America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heads_of_state_of_the_Federal_Republic_of_Central_America) in the 1820s and 1830s.[^cite_note-LATcult-357] *El Faro*'s Gabriel Labrador compared him to 18th-century military officer and Venezuela independence leader [Simón Bolívar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar) for wanting to form a "union of the \[Central American] people".[^cite_note-EPexpans-180]

### Emigration

In an interview with [*VICE News*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_News)' Krishna Andavolu shortly after Bukele's inauguration, he said that he "share\[s] the same concern President Trump \[has with] immigration, but for different reasons \[...] \[Trump] doesn't want our people to go; I don't want our people to leave."[^cite_note-397] In a 2021 interview on [Fox News](11121.md)' *[Tucker Carlson Tonight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tucker_Carlson_Tonight)*, Bukele attributed [mass emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_American_migrant_caravans) from Central America to the United States to the region's "lack of economic opportunity" and "lack of security" and described the level of emigration to the United States as "immoral". He argued that emigration strained the United States and impeded domestic efforts to improve living conditions in El Salvador.[^cite_note-398]

## Public image

### Relationship with the press

> There is a before and an after Nayib Bukele for El Salvador. (*Hay un antes y un después de Nayib Bukele para El Salvador.*)

— *[Forbes Central America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)*, 20 June 2024

[^cite_note-399]

Bukele and a number of his government officials have attacked journalists and news outlets in speeches and on social media.[^cite_note-Pereira-350]<sup>: 29–30 </sup>[^cite_note-Reuters_troll-400] He has dismissed critics of his government as spreading "[fake news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news)"[^cite_note-Reuters_troll-400] and accused them of being "[mercenaries](73374.md)".[^cite_note-HWRlvt-401] Bukele has also stated that journalism was once a "noble career that sought the truth" ("*carrera noble que buscaba la verdad*") that had supposedly become [propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda).[^cite_note-402] Journalists have been harassed and threatened online by Bukele's supporters. The El Salvador Journalists Association (APES) estimated that by November 2022, at least a dozen journalists had fled El Salvador since Bukele took office citing fears for their safety.[^cite_note-Reuters_troll-400] APES has also stated that journalists have experienced threats, harassment, [doxxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doxxing), intimidation, surveillance, and criminal prosecution during Bukele's presidency.[^cite_note-403]

According to political scientists, El Salvador has experienced democratic backsliding under Bukele as he has dismantled democratic institutions, curtailed political and civil liberties, and attacked independent media and the political opposition.[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405] Journalists, politicians, and political analysts have described Bukele as an [autocrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocracy),[^cite_note-BMKB-379][^cite_note-Vox-360][^cite_note-406] an [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian),[^cite_note-TEcau-407][^cite_note-408][^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410] a [strongman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_strongman),[^cite_note-FTCECOT-90][^cite_note-Bergengruen-343][^cite_note-admirers-354][^cite_note-Vox-360] a *[caudillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caudillo)*,[^cite_note-TEcau-407][^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412] and a "[millennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennial) dictator".[^cite_note-popularity-345][^cite_note-413][^cite_note-414] He has ironically referred to himself in his Twitter profile as the "Dictator of El Salvador",[^cite_note-APdic-415] "the coolest dictator in the world"[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417] (although news outlets often render this as the "world's coolest dictator"),[^cite_note-APwcd-348][^cite_note-CNNtbm-367][^cite_note-418] the "[Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor) of El Salvador",[^cite_note-419] the "[CEO](52234.md) of El Salvador",[^cite_note-FTCEO-420] and the "[Philosopher King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosopher_king)".[^cite_note-APwcd-348][^cite_note-421] Eduardo Escobar, a lawyer with Citizen Action, a non-governmental organization, stated that Bukele's use of his Twitter profile was part of his strategy to "ridicule the feelings of the public or the opposition".[^cite_note-APdic-415]

In November 2021, Bukele introduced a bill known as the "Foreign Agents Law" ("*Ley de Agentes Extranjeros*") to the Legislative Assembly with the goal of "prohibiting foreign interference" ("*prohibir la injerencia extranjera*") in Salvadoran political affairs. Although he stated that the law was modeled on the United States' [Foreign Agents Registration Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Agents_Registration_Act), critics instead compared the Foreign Agents Law to Nicaraguan laws that exercise press [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship) by shutting down organizations and arresting journalists.[^cite_note-FAL-227] In April 2022, the Legislative Assembly passed a law that allowed courts to sentence journalists to 10 to 15 years' imprisonment for reproducing or transmitting messages from gangs at the beginning of the country's gang crackdown. The APES described the law as "a clear attempt at censorship of media".[^cite_note-TGagl-422] According to a September 2024 [Infobae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infobae) report, leaked audio recordings made by Alejandro Muyshondt in August 2020 supposedly recorded him and [Ernesto Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernesto_Castro), then Bukele's personal secretary, agreeing to spy on the newspapers *El Diario de Hoy*, *El Faro*, *La Prensa Gráfica*, and *Revista Factum*.[^cite_note-423]

A few weeks after *El Faro* alleged that Bukele's government had negotiated with gangs in 2020 to reduce the country's homicide rate, Bukele launched an investigation of *El Faro* for [money laundering](19390.md).[^cite_note-popularity-345] Although the office of the attorney general did not begin such an investigation, *El Faro* was subject to tax audits that Human Rights Watch's José Miguel Vivanco described as "selective and abusive". The audits were suspended in March 2021 after a Supreme Court order citing concerns about a risk to freedom of expression.[^cite_note-HWRlvt-401] In 2022, [Amnesty International](18947898.md) stated that at least 22 Salvadoran journalists (most of whom worked for *El Faro*) had their phones tapped by the Salvadoran government using the Israeli [Pegasus](51424588.md) spyware.[^cite_note-TGagl-422] *El Faro* moved its headquarters to [San José, Costa Rica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Jos%C3%A9,_Costa_Rica) in April 2023, saying that it was trying to avoid "fabricated accusations" from Bukele's government.[^cite_note-424] In 2025, Bukele claimed that most independent journalists and media outlets were part of a supposed "global money laundering operation" ("*operación mundial de lavado de dinero*"), referring to the [United States Agency for International Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Agency_for_International_Development) (USAID).[^cite_note-425]

### Hosting of international events

R'Bonney Gabriel and Nayib Bukele sitting in chairs, facing each other

Bukele with Miss Universe 2022 [R'Bonney Gabriel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R'Bonney_Gabriel) in October 2023

During Bukele's presidency, El Salvador has hosted a number of international sporting events and one edition of the [Miss Universe](150340.md) beauty pageant. Some experts have described El Salvador's hosting of such events as an attempt at [sportswashing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sportswashing).[^cite_note-sportswashing-426][^cite_note-sportswashing2-427]

Bukele has promoted [surfing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surfing) as part of El Salvador's tourism market. He designated part of El Salvador's [Pacific](23070.md) coastline in the [La Libertad Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Libertad_Department_\(El_Salvador\)) as "Surf City",[^cite_note-428] where the [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_ISA_World_Surfing_Games) and [2023 ISA World Surfing Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_ISA_World_Surfing_Games) were hosted.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430] El Salvador also hosted the [2023 Central American and Caribbean Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Central_American_and_Caribbean_Games). At the tournament's opening ceremony, Bukele rebuked critics by saying that he was "not a dictator" and told them to ask everyday Salvadorans what they thought about his "supposed dictatorship".[^cite_note-sportswashing2-427]

In January 2023, Bukele announced that El Salvador would host the [Miss Universe 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miss_Universe_2023) pageant; the last time El Salvador had hosted Miss Universe was in [1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miss_Universe_1975).[^cite_note-431] At the pageant, Bukele said that Miss Universe had given El Salvador the opportunity to "show the world what we are capable of".[^cite_note-432] On the day of the pageant, 300 members of the Movement for Victims of the State of Emergency held a protest demanding the release of innocent victims of the country's gang crackdown and wanting "Miss Universe to see that Salvadorans are suffering". Some protestors wore [sashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sash) reading "Miss Political Prisoners", "Miss Persecution", and "Miss Mass Trials".[^cite_note-sportswashing-426][^cite_note-MUp-433]

### Job approval and popularity

Main article: [Opinion polling on the Nayib Bukele presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_on_the_Nayib_Bukele_presidency)

A crowd of people in front of the Legislative Assembly of El Salvador holding banners supporting Nayib Bukele and waving to a camera

A crowd of Bukele supporters in front of the Legislative Assembly's meeting room in 2023

Although protests against Bukele occurred in [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Salvadoran_protests) during the COVID-19 pandemic[^cite_note-434] and in [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Salvadoran_protests) about his re-election campaign and gang crackdown,[^cite_note-MUp-433][^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436] he has retained [high job-approval ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_on_the_Nayib_Bukele_presidency) throughout his presidency. Bukele's approval rating has never gone below 75 percent, and has averaged in the 90s.[^cite_note-popularity-345][^cite_note-TEcau-407][^cite_note-437] He is one of the most popular presidents in Salvadoran history,[^cite_note-438] and the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)*' Kate Linthicum called him "one of the most popular leaders in the world".[^cite_note-popularity-345] The [United States Institute of Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Institute_of_Peace)'s Mary Speck referred to Bukele as "Latin America's — and possibly the world's — most popular leader".[^cite_note-439] Risa Grais-Targow, a director at the [Eurasia Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia_Group), described Bukele's approval rating as "sky-high" and "really unprecedented".[^cite_note-FTCEO-420]

In addition to Bukele's domestic popularity, he is also very popular among [Salvadorans living in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvadoran_Americans) and throughout [Latin America](18524.md).[^cite_note-LATcult-357] Some Latin American state leaders and other politicians have sought to emulate his government policies.[^cite_note-admirers-354][^cite_note-LATcult-357] In some countries, such as Colombia and Ecuador, opinion polls found Bukele more popular with their residents than domestic politicians. [Steven Levitsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Levitsky), a political scientist and the director of [Harvard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University)'s Latin American studies center, wrote that "everybody wants to be a Bukele" and compared his popularity across Latin America to that of former Venezuelan president [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez).[^cite_note-LATcult-357] Some political analysts consider Bukele's popularity a [cult of personality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_of_personality).[^cite_note-LATcult-357][^cite_note-440]

## Honors and decorations

Nayib Bukele, wearing the sash of the National Order of Juan Mora Fernández, and Rodrigo Chaves holding a document

Bukele receiving the [National Order of Juan Mora Fernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Order_of_Juan_Mora_Fern%C3%A1ndez?action=edit\&redlink=1) from Costa Rican president [Rodrigo Chaves Robles](70007255.md)

[Beijing International Studies University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_International_Studies_University) awarded Bukele an [honorary doctoral degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honorary_degree) in December 2019.[^cite_note-441] In 2021, *Time* named Bukele as one of the [world's 100 most influential people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100).[^cite_note-442]
